40:- **Reusable multi-lens review skill** — promote `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md` to an lr-dev skill (`lr:dev-review`) usable for any code change. *Survives the reframe.* See draft §6.
71:- **Per-session opt-in gate flag** — `/lr:finalize --review` that restores the old approval flow for sensitive sessions. Cheap to add. Trigger: user asks for it at least twice.
76:- **Compaction-aware narrative quality** — on long sessions, auto-compaction may drop or compress earlier turns; summaries skew toward late-session work and can hallucinate earlier events. Current mitigation: prompt instructs the model to note "early-session hazy" rather than confabulate (weak). Possible directions: landmark scratch file written during the session for summarize to cross-reference; bounded read of the session JSONL isolated to this site; accept bias and rely on in-session summarization cadence. Trigger: systematic bias observed when reviewing the summary corpus.
98:- **Archive raw Claude Code session JSONL files together with our session summaries; make them searchable and attachable.** User-raised 2026-06-05. Today's `sessions/YYYY/MM/<uuid>.md` is a *composed* summary — what the agent thought was worth preserving. The raw `~/.claude/projects/<encoded-cwd>/*.jsonl` files (full transcript: every user message, every tool call, every result, every reasoning trace) are far richer and live outside the agent repo, where they: (a) get cleaned up or rotated by Claude Code without our control, (b) aren't bound to our session UUID — only to Claude's own session id, (c) can't be searched alongside lore content. Proposal shape: at finalize (and at the new "call it a day" suspend, when that lands), **copy the raw JSONL(s) for the active Claude Code session into the agent repo** under a structured location, link them to the session UUID, and expose search/restore. Roughly: (1) **storage location** — `sessions/YYYY/MM/<uuid>.jsonl` sibling of the summary, or `sessions/YYYY/MM/<uuid>/transcript.jsonl` if there'll be more attachments later (logs, generated artifacts, reflection notes); (2) **resolution** — finalize already knows the session UUID it's writing; needs to also resolve "which JSONL files belong to *this* Claude Code session" — likely via the `--parent-session-id` known at boot for teammates, or via the canonical session-id file Claude Code writes (re-confirm by reading `jsonl-session-files-investigation.md`, which we already have); (3) **multi-agent capture** — `/lr:spawn-teammate` sessions: each teammate has its own JSONL; finalize on the lead-side captures the lead's, finalize on the teammate-side captures the teammate's, summary cross-links so a researcher can reconstruct the team conversation; (4) **search surface** — a `/lr:sessions-search <query>` skill that grep/JSONL-parses across archived transcripts, optionally scoped to a date range, an agent, or a session uuid; results return matching messages with their session UUID + summary pointer + line/turn anchor; (5) **archive policy** — JSONLs are large; consider compression (`.jsonl.gz`) for older sessions, possibly an age-out / cold-storage tier; (6) **attach to session feature** — a `/lr:session-attach <session-uuid>` that loads a past session's transcript into context for analysis ("what was I thinking when I designed X?"), composes with the call-it-a-day resume (suspend doc may itself be a thin pointer at the JSONL + a few prose highlights, not a hand-composed seed); (7) **privacy / size considerations** — transcripts may contain secrets, paths, internal links; consider a redaction pass option, repo-size impact (lots of large committed binary-ish files), and gitignore-vs-commit policy (probably commit by default for team-shared knowledge, with an opt-out); (8) **schema evolution** — Claude Code's JSONL schema can change between versions; archived transcripts must record the Claude Code version that produced them so search/parse code can handle multiple schemas. Composes with: `/lr:summarize` (the summary becomes the *index* for the transcript, pointing into specific turns), suspend/resume (the JSONL is the high-fidelity snapshot the suspend doc references), Agent Teams (per-teammate transcripts cross-linked by parent-session-id), code-review/research workflows (search past sessions for "have I solved this before?"). Already-relevant lore: **`jsonl-session-files-investigation.md`** documents what we currently know about the JSONL location/format — start there. Open seams the proposal doesn't yet decide: which finalize phase owns the copy (Phase 4 commit alongside the summary, or a new phase 3.5?), whether to also capture related artifacts (workflow scripts run, agent transcripts from spawned subagents), and whether transcript archival is mandatory for finalize or a per-session opt-in. Status: parked, captured as a deferred lifecycle/observability feature; pairs strongly with the call-it-a-day and boot-context-caching entries above (all three are "session as a first-class durable artifact" ideas). See `jsonl-session-files-investigation.md`, `session-summaries-feature.md`, `finalization-process.md`, `framework-improvements-backlog.md` § Call-It-a-Day, § Autonomous Agents.
106:- **Auto-commit + auto-push the boot-time version upgrade.** User-raised 2026-06-05; designed to draft, then parked as too complicated to ship right now. The motivating failure: a lore agent repo can be locally upgraded (migrations applied, version stamped, committed) but never pushed — leaving the team unaware. Real instance: `activities-lore-agents` carrying unpushed `stamp version 13` + `stamp version 15`. Today's `version-check.md` Step 4 explicitly forbids commits ("user reviews and commits themselves"), which makes this orphan-commit state structurally available at every unattended boot. Proposal shape: after Step 3 (Stamp), add Step 4 (commit, scope = write-set ∪ `lore-repo.md`, never `git add -A`) and Step 5 (`git push --ff-only`, with content-equality reconciliation on push-rejection — fetch, compare remote write-set bytes against ours, on match `reset --soft HEAD~1` + `checkout origin/<branch> -- <write-set>`, on divergence keep local commit + warn). Same scoping applies to the reconciliation checkout (never full `git pull`). Boot must never fail — every commit/push/fetch failure path lands in degraded-mode boot with a warning. Asymmetry with `/lr:update` is preserved (boot is unattended → carries gate + auto-push; `/lr:update` is interactive → user commits/pushes themselves). Open seams: branch detection (`@{u}` resolution + no-upstream fallback), write-set grammar for migration-time deletions/renames so `git add -- <path>` picks them up, identity/network failure paths, ship as a procedural-doc release (cache-clear footer + manifest bump + `versioning-release-types.md` backfill + multi-round multi-lens review). Composes cleanly with the v13 auto-pull (which already defuses the common race). Full draft preserved in `workdir/draft-auto-push-after-upgrade.md` — resume from there. See `freshness-contracts-at-session-boundaries.md` (the principle, mirror of auto-pull on the *push* side), `dirty-tree-gates-write-vs-read-distinction.md` (the write-set discipline this extends), `auto-pull-mechanism.md` (model for graduated network-failure handling).
112:- **A periodic, agent-run deep-restructuring pass over an agent's own knowledge — distinct from per-session reflect/merge.** User-raised 2026-06-13. Per-session merge is *incremental and local* (integrate this session's reflections, preserve-don't-lose) and deliberately does not restructure — like waking working memory. Over many sessions the base drifts: `lore-context.md` bloats toward an index, topics over-granulate, the summary-topic mid-tier weakens, stale topics linger, cross-references rot. This job is the **consolidation force** — the analogue of what the brain does during sleep: stop encoding and reorganize long-term memory structurally. It is the *active mechanism* for the "no garbage-collector" gap (§ Architecture-Review Follow-Ups, subitem 3) and the periodic deep counterpart to the per-session `lore-context.md` shape discipline added to `process-merge.md` Step 4 this session.
121:  - **Safety / blast radius** — high: it restructures and *deletes* team-shared knowledge. Wants a **preview / dry-run** that reports the proposed restructuring before applying (compose with the dry-run-summary discipline), a **grooming report** (what changed + why, before/after size), user review by default until trusted, and the merge "be conservative with deletions" guard applied more strongly. A big reorg is more push-conflict-prone than an incremental merge — consider a "groom when quiet" convention.
123:  Status: parked vision, captured the day the per-session shape discipline landed — they're the incremental / consolidation pair. Promote to its own lore topic + `workdir/` draft when active. See § Architecture-Review Follow-Ups (subitem 3), `process-merge.md` Step 4, `autonomous-agents-vision.md`, `consistency-checks.md`, `naming-foundational-principles.md`.
127:- **Post-merge diff verification** — an outside architecture review (2026-07-02) flagged merge as the framework's highest-risk operation with no automated safety net: a bad `lore-context.md` compaction, or a reflection that silently overwrites/contradicts an existing topic, is currently only caught if a human happens to read the `git diff`. Nothing checks it automatically. Proposed shape: after a merge subagent finishes (still pre-commit, since merge already leaves changes uncommitted), spawn a second subagent — booted with the same agent context — whose only job is to adversarially review that agent's `git diff` in the working tree and answer: was anything lost, contradicted, or made unreachable from `lore-context.md`'s summary references? Deliberately **per-merge and cheap**, distinct from the "sleep pass" housekeeping job above, which is periodic and deep — the two are complementary in the same way merge (incremental) and the sleep pass (consolidation) already are. Not designed yet — this is a backlog capture, not a spec. Natural first cut: ship as an opt-in `/lr:finalize --verify-merge` flag before making it the default, consistent with how other safety gates in this framework started opt-in. See `process-merge.md` Step 4 (the compaction step most likely to lose information), § Lore Housekeeping / the "sleep" pass above.
129:### Architecture-Review Follow-Ups (2026-06-13)
131:- **Framework simplification / subtraction pass** — cross-cutting theme from the 2026-06-13 architecture-review session: the framework optimizes for accretion and preservation with no countervailing *subtraction* force. Three subitems, surfaced together; consider as a unit when a simplification session is warranted.
134:  - **"Name every principle" has no garbage-collector.** `naming-foundational-principles.md` grows named disciplines monotonically → `role.md` bullets → `/lr:check` checks, with no counter-force; `delete-don't-mark` handles *wrongness*, not *redundancy / over-granularity*. Add a periodic *consolidation* discipline that folds sibling / over-granular clusters (e.g. the `verify-before-*` trio, the `feedback-*` family). Upstream of the lore-context shape-drift discussed separately (review item #1). See `naming-foundational-principles.md`.
140:- **Surface topic staleness at recall time** — an outside architecture review (2026-07-02) noted a gap between design intent and actual behavior: `system-design-principles.md` treats "git tracks metadata" as covering creation/update history, but no procedure actually *reads* that metadata back at the point where it matters — when an agent recalls a topic and treats its contents as current. A 14-month-old operational recommendation currently reads identically to one written yesterday. Proposed shape: in `lore-search.md`, when a search surfaces a topic, run `git log -1 --format=%cs -- <topic-path>` and include the date alongside the result, with a soft flag (not a hard warning) on topics older than some threshold (needs a number — start with a round default like 6 months and adjust from real use). ### 32. Assistant — 2026-07-25T00:47:58.574Z

- model: `claude-opus-5`

Strong idea, and here's the thing you should know first: **you've already designed most of this, twice.** It's in my lore as accumulated operating knowledge, not as a feature.

- `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md` — my own three-lens fan-out discipline, hardened across the v11–v28 ships (v15 took 7 rounds to converge, v18 caught a real hang, the 2026-07-17 rebrand review ran 4 rounds with persistent reviewers).
- `framework-improvements-backlog.md` line 40: *"Reusable multi-lens review skill — promote `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md` to a skill usable for any code change."*
- `workdir/draft-lr-dev.md` §5, from your 2026-06-01 design dialogue: *"The user's three-subagent independent review (lenses chosen by the orchestrator; address-then-decide-if-another-round) **is** my `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md`."*

So this isn't a greenfield design — it's promoting hard-won agent-level knowledge into a plugin-level capability. That's the good kind of feature: the spec is mostly transcription of what already works.

## Your three questions

**Skill, not command.** "Command" isn't a framework-level concept — it's an engine's *surface* for a skill. One `skills/review/SKILL.md` becomes `/lr:review` on Claude, `/lr-review` on Cursor (generated wrapper), `$lr:review` on Codex. Engine-independence comes free from the existing convention: the SKILL.md is a **thin one-line pointer** to `docs/review.md`, where all logic lives, and the doc says *"fan out per your engine profile's `subagent-spawn` binding"* rather than naming the `Agent` tool.

**Single skill with params — yes**, and your context worry is already solved by that pattern: skill bodies are one-liners, so 30 skills cost near-nothing until invoked. Adding a second skill would be the mistake; params are right.

**Layer: core plugin, not the DF module.** The backlog filed it as `lr:dev-review` under lr-dev, but that's wrong — the pattern is domain-agnostic (I use it on doc sweeps and lore edits, not just code). Ship it as core `/lr:review`; DF/AIQA becomes its first consumer.

## Three things in your flow I'd sharpen

**1. Termination can't be the host's call alone.** In your design the same session writes the fixes *and* decides the concerns are negligible — that's self-graded convergence, the weakest joint. Cheap fix: every reviewer ends with a mandated verdict line (`SHIP` / `SHIP-WITH-FIXES` / `BLOCK`), and the loop **may not terminate while any reviewer's last-round verdict is `BLOCK`** — the host can still override, but must say so out loud. Plus a hard `--rounds` cap (default 3) so a bad loop can't burn 9 agents unattended.

**2. Fresh reviewers each round will re-raise what you rejected.** This is empirically the load-bearing mechanic: on the 4-round rebrand review, *no reviewer re-raised a settled item* — because each round's brief opened with a numbered **APPLIED / DECLINED(+reason)** ledger of that lens's own prior findings. With fresh spawns (the engine-portable default) that digest is mandatory, not optional. Related: expect round N's findings to cluster on round N−1's *edits* — fixes create new seams; that's normal, not failure.

**3. Bias isolation needs to be a stated rule, not a hope.** Two concrete constraints: (a) reviewers must be **plain subagents, never context-inheriting forks** — on Claude that means explicitly *not* `subagent_type: "fork"`; (b) the brief gives the reviewer the change's **goal** (so it can judge fitness) but **not your rationale for each choice** — rationale pre-empts the criticism you're paying for. That second one is a genuinely new distinction, not in my lore yet; if it survives the design it earns a topic name.

## Also worth folding in from lore (free wins)

- Round 1 and the final round are **different jobs**: R1 = breadth, parallel, lens-isolated. Final = depth, *single* reviewer, full diff, filesystem-verifying — it catches cross-doc drift that per-lens reviewers structurally cannot.
- **Lenses must be mutually exclusive**, and each brief must say *what to skip because another lens owns it*. Overlapping lenses waste a slot.
- **The don't-fan-out test:** "would one agent looking at all N items produce the same verdicts?" If yes, batch. A DF prototype burned 19 parallel agents on a uniform property and dropped zero findings.
- **Partial returns are additive evidence** — a stalled reviewer that returned one finding did useful work; don't abort the round.
- Reviewers hallucinate: **verify each BLOCKER/HIGH against the actual file before fixing.**

## Sketch

```
/lr:review [scope] [--lenses N|"a,b,c"] [--rounds N] [--report-only] [--model tier]
```
Default: scope = uncommitted working-tree diff; 3 auto-chosen lenses; iterate to convergence, cap 3 rounds. `--report-only` gives you exactly the "basic simplest flow" you described first (one round, no fixes) — which is also the honest degraded mode.

## Two decisions I need from you

1. **Cursor.** Its engine profile deliberately forbids claiming subagent fan-out — the binding says *"execute host-side, serially."* For every other skill that's a graceful degradation; here it **destroys the feature**, because a host reviewing its own changes is precisely the bias you're removing. Options: (a) `/lr:review` declares Cursor unsupported until a native mechanism is validated, (b) it degrades to `--report-only` with a loud "reviewer is not independent on this engine" warning, (c) we spend a session probing Cursor for a real subagent primitive. I lean **(a)** — an honest gap beats a fake independent reviewer.
2. **Scope of the ship.** Minimal core skill (this + docs + cursor wrapper + `/lr:check` entry + manifests), or also wire DF/AIQA to consume it? I'd ship core-only first.

Want me to write the draft to `workdir/draft-review-skill.md`, or keep talking it through?

### 43. Assistant — 2026-07-25T00:52:48.164Z

- model: `claude-opus-5`

Nothing is wrong with Cursor. The forbiddance is **self-imposed epistemic honesty, not a discovered limitation** — and I wrote it. From `docs/engines/cursor.md`:

> *"No verified Cursor-native in-session subagent mechanism is relied on by the framework… Do not claim parallel fan-out on Cursor until it is validated on the real engine."*
> *"This profile is intentionally conservative. It favors a verified host-side path over unverified claims about Cursor-native subagents."*

## Why it reads stronger than it is

It's "**never probed**", not "probed and absent." I just grepped the Cursor probe notes (`cursor-agent-cli-probe-findings.md`, `cursor-cli-and-harness-operational-notes.md`): **zero mentions** of subagent/spawn/parallel. The question was never asked.

Contrast Codex, where it *was* asked: we probed, found native `spawn_agent`/`wait_agent`, and validated fan-out end-to-end on real `codex exec`. Cursor got the conservative route because at v20 (2026-07-05) the shipped goal was deliberately "a small, explicit engine-profile diff over a native-feeling redesign" — and the serial override was **sufficient**: 19/19 lifecycle scenarios green on real Cursor. No reason to spend a probe.

The profile even says out loud that Cursor probably *does* have the capability — the parallel-worktrees gate notes *"Cursor has its own worktree support and broader parallel-agent features in the product, but the framework has not yet validated how that interacts with Lore's workspace invariant."*

## But here's the part that actually matters for your skill

In every existing fan-out site — recall, consult, merge — the subagent is an **optimization**: parallelism plus context isolation. Serial host-side execution gets the same *answer*, just slower. That's why the conservative override was lossless.

`/lr:review` is the first skill where the subagent is the **semantics**. Independence from the author's bias *is* the deliverable. A degradation that's free everywhere else becomes feature-destroying here — the host reviewing its own work isn't a slow review, it's not a review.

That distinction isn't in my lore yet and it's load-bearing for this design (candidate topic: `subagent-as-optimization-vs-subagent-as-semantics.md`).

## Empirical check, just now

`cursor-agent 2026.07.20-8cc9c0b`'s CLI surface has **no spawn/subagent flag** — it has `--worktree`, `create-chat`, `worker`, `resume`. But that doesn't settle it either: Codex's `spawn_agent` is a *model-facing in-session tool*, and it wouldn't appear in `codex --help` either. So in-session spawn on Cursor remains formally unprobed.

## Which changes my recommendation — I'll retract lean (a)

Reviewers don't need to be **subagents**. They need to be **independent contexts**. And every engine has a verified headless one-shot:

| Engine | Independent invocation | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Claude Code | in-session `Agent` (cheapest) | verified |
| Codex | native `spawn_agent` | verified end-to-end |
| Cursor | `cursor-agent -p "<brief>" --output-format json` | verified surface — the lifecycle harness already drives it |

A shell-out to a fresh engine process is independence in its purest form — *more* isolated than an in-session subagent, not less. So define the skill's primitive as **"an independent engine invocation," preferring the engine's native subagent where one is verified, falling back to headless shell-out.** Cursor becomes supported with no unverified claim and no profile upgrade.

Better still, the machinery already exists: the **Being Keeper spawns real headless `cursor-agent` sessions today**, and its contract is documented in `cursor-agent-real-invocation-contract.md` — including the gotcha that Cursor is cost-blind (no `total_cost_usd`, hence the flat `--session-cost-usd` fallback) and that its sandboxed shell `setsid`s children out of process-group kills. Reuse that, don't rediscover it.

Caveats worth stating in the doc: shell-out needs a logged-in CLI, is subject to Cursor's approval/sandbox gates, and costs real money outside the host session's accounting. On Claude the in-session `Agent` stays strictly better — so this is a fallback, not the default.

So the Cursor answer is: **not unsupported, and not a fake reviewer either** — a third path neither of my earlier options considered.

Want me to draft this now? I'd write `workdir/draft-review-skill.md` covering the primitive definition, params, the round protocol with the dispositions ledger, and reviewer-gated termination.

2	
3	When the user invokes `/lr:plain-language`, adopt this communication style for the rest of the
4	session (until they turn it off), and re-assert it now if you had drifted.
5	
6	The user reads English as a second language. Dense, compressed prose costs them real effort — they
7	have to read it two or three times to understand it. Write so they get it on the **first** read:
8	
9	- Use everyday words and short sentences.
10	- One idea per sentence. Don't pack several ideas into one line.
11	- Explain things in a straightforward order, step by step.
12	- Stay concise — but "concise" means **cutting extra words, not compressing ideas together** until
13	  they are hard to parse. Short *and clear* beats short *and dense*.
14	- Self-check: if a sentence would need to be read twice to be understood, it is too compressed.
15	  Rewrite it.
16	
17	This does **not** conflict with being brief. Keep replies short and say only what matters right now;
18	just say those things plainly.
19

21

26	## Related skills

30	each one as you need it.

Based on the search results, here's information about Cursor agent subagents:

## What are Subagents?

Subagents are independent agents specialized to handle discrete parts of a parent agent's task. They run in parallel, use their own context, and can be configured with custom prompts, tool access, and models.

## Key Features

**Parallel Execution:** Subagents run in parallel to explore your codebase, with each one using the best model for the task.

**Async Support:** Subagents can now run asynchronously, allowing the parent to continue working while subagents run in the background. Subagents can also spawn their own subagents, creating a tree of coordinated work.

## Using Subagents

### Automatic Delegation
Agent proactively delegates tasks based on the task complexity and scope, custom subagent descriptions in your project, and current context and available tools.

### Explicit Invocation
You can request a specific subagent by using the `/name` syntax in your prompt, or by mentioning them naturally in your message.

### Nesting Limitations
The top-level agent and its direct subagents can launch subagents, but a subagent launched by another subagent can't launch further ones.

You can find the official documentation at [cursor.com/docs/subagents](https://cursor.com/docs/subagents).

Based on the search results, here's what I found about Cursor CLI subagents and spawning parallel agents:

## Subagents Overview

Subagents are independent agents specialized to handle discrete parts of a parent agent's task. They run in parallel, use their own context, and can be configured with custom prompts, tool access, and models.

## Spawning Parallel Agents in Cursor CLI

The Cursor CLI can spawn subagents by calling cursor-agent in headless mode via shell commands. Subagents are recursive agents where you can apply different prompts and models depending on the task.

## Build in Parallel Feature

Cursor 3.3 (May 7, 2026) introduced "Build in Parallel": spawn N subagents from one prompt, each tackling a different sub-task, results merged.

## Using Git Worktrees for Parallel Execution

A practical approach for running subagents in parallel is to use git worktrees. Cursor creates worktrees, then runs agents in separate worktrees. Each agent only modifies files in its worktree, so no cross-editing conflicts occur. Multiple agents work at the same time in separate worktrees.

## Note on CLI Documentation

It's worth noting that according to discussions in the community, documentation for CLI subagents appears to be limited compared to the IDE implementation, with some users reporting that subagents execute serially rather than in parallel when using the CLI.

**Initial Finding:** Subagents were **not working in Cursor CLI**. Users reported the Task tool—required to dispatch subagents—was unavailable, causing agents to execute sequentially in a single context instead of launching parallel, isolated subagents.

**User Experience:** One developer stated: *"The Task tool is not injected into the CLI agent's toolset"* and that this appeared to be a CLI-specific limitation not present in the IDE.

**Final Status:** The feature was successfully enabled. A user confirmed: *"I can confirm that the Task tool is available to Cursor now and I'm able to use sub-agents on CLI."*

**Conclusion:** Subagents now work in CLI with parallel execution capability, though this required a product fix.

# Subagent Definition and Invocation

## File Format & Location

Subagents are markdown files with YAML frontmatter. They're stored in:
- **Project scope**: `.cursor/agents/`, `.claude/agents/`, or `.codex/agents/`
- **User scope**: `~/.cursor/agents/`, `~/.claude/agents/`, or `~/.codex/agents/`

Key frontmatter fields:
- `name`: identifier (lowercase, hyphens)
- `description`: determines when Agent delegates
- `model`: `inherit` or specific model ID with optional parameters
- `readonly`: restrict write permissions
- `is_background`: run without blocking parent

## Invocation Methods

**Explicit**: Use `/name` syntax or natural language mentions
**Automatic**: Agent proactively delegates based on task complexity and subagent descriptions
**Parallel**: Multiple subagents launch concurrently via single Task tool calls

## Task Tool & Spawning

The documentation indicates agents use the Task tool to spawn subagents, though it doesn't explicitly detail mechanics. "Each subagent execution returns an agent ID" for resuming later.

## CLI & Availability

The docs mention subagents work in "the editor, CLI, and Cloud Agents," confirming cursor-agent CLI support.

## Technical Constraints

**Nesting limits**: Subagents can launch child subagents, but "a subagent launched by another subagent can't launch further ones."

**Parallelism**: No hard limit stated; token costs scale with concurrent execution.

**Model selection**: Admin restrictions, plan limitations, or legacy Max Mode requirements override configured models.

**Tool access**: Subagents inherit parent tools; cloud subagents use team-configured MCP servers only.
```

### 65. Assistant — 2026-07-25T01:03:04.513Z

- model: `claude-opus-5`

In one line: **review the changes from three independent perspectives, fix what matters, repeat until it is clean.**

The steps:

1. You point it at a set of changes.
2. This session picks the **three perspectives** that matter most for *these* changes.
3. It spawns **three subagents** — one per perspective. They did not make the changes, so they carry no bias from our work.
4. Each one reviews and reports findings.
5. This session **triages** each finding: accept or reject, with a reason. Rejecting is allowed.
6. It fixes what it accepted.
7. New round. Three fresh reviewers, told what was applied and what was declined and why.
8. Stop when a round finds nothing worth fixing. A round cap protects against loops.

What you get back: the improved changes, plus a short ledger of every finding and its verdict.

The core idea in one word: **independence**. The reviewer must not be the author.

#### Result

````text
1	# Worktrees
2	
3	The lore framework's convention for working with non-default branches of repos inside a workspace.
4	
5	## Invariant
6	
7	Top-level directories in `<workspace>/` that are checkouts of git repos **stay on their default branch**. The default branch is whatever that repo treats as production state — commonly `main`, but `master`, `trunk`, or any other name is equally valid. Git itself tracks the default per-repo; the framework does not maintain its own list.
8	
9	The invariant matters because:
10	
11	- The workspace is read as a snapshot of current production across all its repos. Switching a top-level checkout to a feature branch breaks that view.
12	- Agent knowledge about "what this repo looks like in prod" assumes the top-level checkout reflects prod. Drift from that assumption quietly corrupts lore.
13	- `/lr:workspace-pull` refreshes all top-level repos. If one is on a feature branch, that refresh semantics gets confusing.
14	
15	## Rule
16	
17	Any work that requires a non-default branch — new features, bug fixes, or just checking out someone else's branch to inspect it — happens in a **git worktree** rooted at:
18	
19	```
20	<workspace>/.worktrees/<repo>/<slug>/
21	```
22	
23	Where:
24	
25	- `<repo>` is the repo directory name (matches the top-level checkout).
26	- `<slug>` is a short, lowercase, hyphen-separated identifier for this particular line of work.
27	
28	Git worktree mechanics are standard; no framework commands wrap them. Typical usage:
29	
30	**New feature work:**
31	
32	```bash
33	cd <workspace>/<repo>
34	git worktree add ../.worktrees/<repo>/<slug> -b <branch-name>
35	```
36	
37	**Inspecting someone else's branch:**
38	
39	```bash
40	cd <workspace>/<repo>
41	git fetch origin
42	git worktree add ../.worktrees/<repo>/<slug> origin/<existing-branch>
43	```
44	
45	**Removing when done:**
46	
47	```bash
48	git worktree remove ../.worktrees/<repo>/<slug>
49	```
50	
51	A branch-naming suggestion — `<agent-name>/<slug>` — signals which agent owns the work in multi-agent workspaces. It's a convention, not enforced.
52	
53	## Optional: Tracking Inflight Worktrees
54	
55	An agent that wants to keep notes on its inflight worktrees can use:
56	
57	```
58	<lore-agent-repo>/agents/<agent-name>/worktrees/<slug>.md
59	```
60	
61	What goes in those notes — structure, skeleton, cadence, whether to archive or delete on prune — is the agent's call. Inspection-grade worktrees may warrant no note; long-lived contributions may warrant a living doc with context, progress, and decisions.
62	
63	This pattern is not mandated and not scaffolded. The framework neither creates these files nor checks for their presence.
64	
65	## What's Out of Scope
66	
67	These are deliberately not part of the framework — they belong in agent lore or repo-specialist agents reached via `/lr:consult` or `/lr:attach`:
68	
69	- PR/MR workflow, review conventions, merge policies
70	- Commit message conventions, signoff requirements
71	- CI/CD interactions
72	- Repo-specific branch protection rules
73	- Worktree cleanup discipline (when to prune, what to preserve)
74	
75	The reasoning is the framework's universal-vs-specific split: git worktrees are universal; everything around them is repo-specific.
76	
77	## Enforcement
78	
79	Convention-only. The framework does not currently check whether top-level repo dirs have drifted onto non-default branches. If that drift becomes a real problem in practice, a `/lr:check` warning is a cheap future addition.
80	
````

#### Result

````text
1	# Consult
2	
3	`/lr:consult` asks an unloaded lore agent a question. A subagent boots the consultant, searches its lore, synthesizes a response, and exits. The host gets back the synthesis plus pointers to specific lore topics or workdir tools worth using directly. The consultant itself is unaffected — no reflection, no merge.
4	
5	This is the lightweight option in the cross-agent-collaboration trio:
6	
7	- `/lr:recall` — search lore of agents already loaded (host + guests)
8	- `/lr:consult` — one-shot question to an unloaded agent; handover of specific files
9	- `/lr:attach` — load another agent for sustained co-work
10	
11	Use consult when a focused question will get you what you need. If you realize you need sustained engagement with the consultant's knowledge across many turns, escalate with `/lr:attach <same-agent>`.
12	
13	## Usage
14	
15	- `/lr:consult <agent-name>` — contextual consult; the host builds the brief from the current session
16	- `/lr:consult <agent-name> <hint>` — focused consult; the hint layers on top of session context
17	
18	## Procedure
19	
20	### Step 1: Preconditions
21	
22	1. **Host must be loaded.** Consult needs the current session as raw material for the brief. If no agent is booted, respond: `No agent loaded. Run /lr:boot <agent-name> first.` and stop.
23	2. **Consultant must exist.** Standard discovery: scan directories for `lore-repo.md`, look for `agents/<name>/role.md`. If not found, list available agents and stop.
24	3. **Do not consult the host.** If the requested name equals the host, respond: `<name> is the host — use /lr:recall for its lore.` and stop.
25	4. **If the consultant is already an attached guest**, point to recall: `<name> is already attached as a guest — use /lr:recall to search its lore alongside the host's.` and stop. Do not dispatch a consult subagent for an already-loaded agent; recall is strictly better.
26	
27	### Step 2: Build the consult brief
28	
29	The brief has four parts, ≤400 words total:
30	
31	1. **Task** — one or two sentences on what the host is actively working on this session.
32	2. **Session context** — two to five bullets: decisions already made, constraints surfaced, files being touched, the specific blocker driving the consult.
33	3. **Question / angle** — the specific question or issue. If the user provided a hint (`$ARGUMENTS` has more than just the agent name), center the angle on it.
34	4. **Output shape** — ask the subagent for:
35	   - A direct answer (≤400 words)
36	   - Pointers to specific **lore topics** worth reading in full — give absolute paths (file paths under `agents/<consultant>/lore/`)
37	   - Pointers to specific **workdir tools, recipes, or scripts** if relevant — give absolute paths
38	   - Optional recommended next steps for the host
39	   - Total consultant response ≤600 words plus pointer lists
40	   - `"Nothing relevant in my lore"` if the consultant genuinely has nothing — do not fabricate relevance
41	
42	Keep the brief itself concise. The subagent will boot and read the consultant's lore — it does not need the host's full lore summarized, only what's in *this* session.
43	
44	### Step 3: Dispatch the subagent
45	
46	> **Engine note.** If your engine profile (`<framework-root>/docs/engines/<engine>.md`) defines a subagent-spawn override, use it instead of the Claude `general-purpose` subagent — e.g. on Codex, `spawn_agent` (role `worker`), with the resolved `<framework-root>` absolute path inlined into the prompt.
47	
48	Use a general-purpose subagent. Prompt shape:
49	
50	> You are being invoked as a short consult. Another lore agent is the host of the current session; they need your help with a focused question.
51	>
52	> **Step A — Boot as `<consultant-name>`.** Read `<framework-root>/docs/agent-boot.md` and follow the boot procedure to load yourself as agent `<consultant-name>`. This includes the standard version reconcile — do that normally; return any release notes text in your final response so the host can surface them to the user.
53	>
54	> **Step B — Answer the consult.** Here is the brief:
55	>
56	> ```
57	> <4-part brief from Step 2>
58	> ```
59	>
60	> Search your lore using the subagent-scan pattern described in `<framework-root>/docs/lore-search.md` (nested Explore dispatch is fine) or direct tools where a targeted lookup is better. Synthesize a response in the requested output shape.
61	>
62	> **Step C — Return.** Send back the synthesis with file pointers. Do not reflect. Do not merge. Do not write anything. Your role ends with this response.
63	
64	The subagent's own context absorbs the boot, migration output, and lore scan. The host sees only the final synthesis returned.
65	
66	### Step 4: Present to the user and the host
67	
68	1. **Relay release notes** (if any came back from Step A's version reconcile) to the user verbatim.
69	2. **Show the consultant's synthesis** to the user, labeled with the consultant's name so it's clear whose voice this is.
70	3. **Read pointed-to files in the host's main context** only if they're load-bearing for the task at hand — otherwise leave them referenced but unread. Over-reading defeats the point of consult being lightweight.
71	4. **Carry the consult's answer** forward as working context for the rest of the session.
72	
73	### Step 5: What the host may and may not do afterwards
74	
75	The consult creates a *handover* of specific knowledge, not a full loading.
76	
77	**May:**
78	- Read any specific files the consultant pointed to (lore topics, workdir tools, recipes, scripts). Workspace visibility makes this free.
79	- Run or adapt workdir tools the consultant recommended.
80	- Apply the consultant's advice directly.
81	
82	**May not:**
83	- Dispatch further lore-search subagents against the consultant's lore. That is attach territory — consult's boundary is the specific files the response called out.
84	- Expect `/lr:recall` to include the consultant's lore afterwards. Recall only covers agents currently loaded (host + attached guests). The consultant was never loaded into the host's session.
85	
86	If either of those limits is too tight for the task, **escalate**: `/lr:attach <consultant-name>` loads the consultant for sustained use.
87	
88	## No finalization for the consultant
89	
90	The consultant does not reflect or merge because of this consult. From the consultant's perspective, the consult is read-only — its lore is queried, nothing is written back. If the host learns something during the session that the consultant should eventually know, the user can boot the consultant directly in a future session to capture it there. A formal consult-feedback mechanism is out of scope for v4.
91	
92	## See also
93	
94	- `<framework-root>/docs/attach.md` — the heavyweight sibling (sustained loading, finalization participation)
95	- `<framework-root>/docs/recall.md` — search lore of already-loaded agents
96	- `<framework-root>/docs/lore-search.md` — search brief structure and mechanics
97	- `<framework-root>/docs/agent-boot.md` — the boot procedure the subagent follows in Step A
98	
````

#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Generate .cursor-skills/lr-*/SKILL.md wrappers from canonical skills/*/SKILL.md.

Cursor uses the skill folder name in the picker (no plugin namespace like Claude's /lr:).
Each wrapper is a thin pointer with lr- prefixed identity; logic stays in docs/*.md.

Run from the framework root after adding or renaming a canonical skill:
  python3 scripts/sync-cursor-skills
"""

from __future__ import annotations

import re
import sys
from pathlib import Path

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
SKILLS = ROOT / "skills"
CURSOR = ROOT / ".cursor-skills"
LEGACY_CURSOR = SKILLS / "cursor"


def transform_description(desc: str, skill_name: str) -> str:
    or_skill = f"lr-{skill_name}"
    desc = re.sub(rf"/lr:{re.escape(skill_name)}\b", f"/{or_skill}", desc)
    desc = re.sub(r"/lr:([\w-]+)", r"/lr-\1", desc)
    return desc


def parse_skill(content: str) -> tuple[list[str], str]:
    lines = content.splitlines(keepends=True)
    if not lines or lines[0].strip() != "---":
        raise ValueError("SKILL.md missing YAML frontmatter")
    fm: list[str] = []
    i = 1
    while i < len(lines):
        if lines[i].strip() == "---":
            return fm, "".join(lines[i + 1 :]).lstrip("\n")
        fm.append(lines[i])
        i += 1
    raise ValueError("SKILL.md frontmatter not closed")


def rebuild_frontmatter(fm: list[str], skill_name: str) -> list[str]:
    lr_name = f"lr-{skill_name}"
=== README skills ===
125:| `/lr:recall [hint]` | Search lore across loaded agents |
127:| `/lr:consult <agent> [hint]` | Ask an unloaded agent a one-off question |
167:| `/lr:doctor` | Diagnose framework runtime issues |
```

#### Result

````text
# Conventions

## Naming

- **Agent names**: lowercase kebab-case (e.g., `code-reviewer`, `data-analyst`)
- **Lore topic filenames**: lowercase kebab-case, descriptive (e.g., `api-retry-behavior.md`)
- **Role update reflections**: prefixed with `role-update-` (e.g., `role-update-scope-change.md`)
- **Plugin skills**: `/lr:<action>` (e.g., `/lr:boot`, `/lr:reflect`) on Claude Code — canonical folders `skills/<action>/`
- **Cursor plugin skills**: `/lr-<action>` (e.g., `/lr-boot`) — prefixed wrappers `.cursor-skills/lr-<action>/`, loaded via `.cursor-plugin/plugin.json`. Regenerate with `scripts/sync-cursor-skills` after changing canonical skills. See `docs/engines/cursor.md`, `/lr:check` #21.
- **Per-agent shortcuts**: optional, generated by `/lr:register-agent` or `/lr:register-repo` — `/lr-<agent-name>-agent` on Claude Code and Cursor, `$lr-<agent-name>-agent` on Codex

## Placeholder Vocabulary

Framework docs use a small set of angle-bracket placeholders. The surrounding text always establishes which specific value substitutes — placeholders are slots, not identities. A workspace with multiple lore agent repos simply iterates, substituting the current value per step.

- **`<workspace>`** — the parent directory containing one or more lore agent repos (and any other repositories they declare). The user runs their coding agent from here. Introduced in v11; this is the term to use in new docs.
- **`<lore-agent-repo>`** — filesystem path to a lore agent repo (directory that contains `lore-repo.md`). Used for repo-scoped operations (`git -C <lore-agent-repo>`, path references). In iterating flows (`/lr:check`, `/lr:update`), substitutes the current iteration's repo. In single-repo flows (`/lr:boot`, `/lr:reflect`), substitutes that repo. Conceptually: a single **domain** of agents.
- **`<guest-lore-agent-repo>`** — same as `<lore-agent-repo>` but specifically identifies the guest being attached (vs the host repo) in `/lr:attach`.
- **`<agent-name>`** — the kebab-case name of an agent (matches the directory name under `agents/`).
- **`<domain>`** — *legacy* placeholder retired in v11; no longer used in framework docs. The parent-dir concept is now `<workspace>`; the conceptual scope of an agent repo is "domain" in prose (no brackets). If you encounter `<domain>` in older release notes or third-party material, read it as `<workspace>`.
- **`<framework-root>`** — the install path of the `lr` plugin. Resolve it per the active engine profile; do not hardcode.

The "lore" prefix on repo placeholders is intentional: it distinguishes lore agent repos from other repos that may live in the same workspace (application code, unrelated tools, etc.).

## Migration / Release-Note Authoring

Every `migrations/<N>.md` and `release-notes/<N>.md` whose changes touch **plugin-cached state** must include a **Clear Plugin Cache** section near the top — right after the Summary, before the detailed "What's New" / "What Changed" body. The reader has just learned *that* something changed; the cache-clear is the single mandatory action and must not be buried at the bottom of a long doc where it is easy to miss. (`release-notes/12.md` and `13.md` are worked examples — footer hoisted directly under the Summary.)

Triggers — include the footer if the version added, removed, renamed, or modified any of:

- A skill (`skills/<name>/SKILL.md` or its directory; on Cursor also `.cursor-skills/lr-<name>/`)
- A `/lr-*` slash command surface
- Any doc referenced by a SKILL.md whose runtime behavior is changed (the cache holds these too)
- A bundled script under `scripts/`
- A bundled MCP server (a root `.mcp.json` or an inline `mcpServers` block in `plugin.json`)

Skip the footer only when the version is **purely informational or content-only** (lore-context conventions, doc rephrasing that doesn't change runtime behavior, framing-only release notes).

Canonical wording (adapt as needed but keep the structure — substitute the `<...>` slots before publishing; use `release-notes/12.md` as a worked example):

```markdown
## Clear Plugin Cache

This release <added/removed/renamed> skills, so the existing plugin cache must be refreshed before Claude Code will see the changes.

After applying the migration:

1. `rm -rf ~/.claude/plugins/cache/lore-framework/` (targeted; preserves other plugins' caches), or `rm -rf ~/.claude/plugins/cache/` (broader fallback), or run the workspace's `./clear_plugin_cache.sh` if present.
2. Exit your current Claude Code session and start a new one.
3. Verify the <new/renamed> skill appears in the available-skills list.

If you skip this step, the prior version's skill catalog will continue to load until the cache is refreshed. See `docs/doctor-stale-plugin-cache.md` for diagnosis.
```

Rationale: the cache-stale failure mode is invisible until the user invokes a missing skill and gets confused. Authoring discipline puts the fix in the migration's own line of sight rather than relying on the user finding `/lr:doctor` after the fact.

## Teammate Discipline
````

| `/lr:workspace-pull` | Pull the workspace repo, clone declared repos, and pull all top-level repos |
| `/lr:pull-lore` | Refresh just the active agents' repos mid-session |
| `/lr:workspace-init` | Bootstrap or refresh the workspace (descriptor, git root, memory file) |
| `/lr:list-agents` | List all agents in the workspace |
| `/lr:list-repos` | List all agent repos in the workspace |

**Working with agents**
| Skill | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `/lr:boot <name>` | Load a lore agent by name |
| `/lr:recall [hint]` | Search lore across loaded agents |
| `/lr:attach <agent>` | Attach another agent as a guest in this session |
| `/lr:consult <agent> [hint]` | Ask an unloaded agent a one-off question |
| `/lr:spawn-teammate [<agent>...]` | **BETA** — Spawn lore agents as Agent Teams teammates |

**Interaction style**
| Skill | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `/lr:dialogue` | Talk in short conversational turns, one step at a time |
| `/lr:follow-me` | Follow your lead — offer small suggestions without racing ahead |
| `/lr:plain-language` | Switch to plain, simple English |

**Background / headless**
| Skill | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `/lr:wait` | Pause until an external event arrives, or sleep — for background / `claude -p` agents |
| `/lr:being [subcommand]` | **BETA** — Manage Lore Beings and the Being Keeper from one entry point |

**Session lifecycle**
| Skill | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `/lr:reflect` | Extract session knowledge into reflections |
| `/lr:merge` | Integrate reflections into lore |
| `/lr:summarize` | Write a session summary |
| `/lr:finalize` | Run full session finalization (reflect + merge + summarize + commit + push) |
| `/lr:takeover [session]` | **BETA** — Continue a session recorded by another engine |

**Authoring agents and repos**
| Skill | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `/lr:create-repo <name>` | Scaffold a new agent repo |
| `/lr:create-agent [name]` | Add a new agent to a repo |
| `/lr:register-agent [repo] <agent>` | Generate one direct boot shortcut |
| `/lr:register-repo <name>` | Generate direct boot shortcuts for every agent in a repo |
| `/lr:unregister-agent [repo] <agent>` | Remove one direct boot shortcut |
| `/lr:unregister-repo <name>` | Remove direct boot shortcuts for every agent in a repo |

**Maintenance**
| Skill | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `/lr:update` | Apply pending framework migrations |
| `/lr:check` | Run consistency checks |

  "content": "# Draft — `/lr:trilens-loop`\n\nDesign record for the three-lens iterated review skill. Implementation lands in\n`lore-architect/trilens-loop` (plugin) + `lore-architect/trilens-loop-dev` (tests).\n\n## 1. What it is\n\n**Review the changes from three independent perspectives, fix what matters, repeat until clean.**\n\n1. Point it at a set of changes (default: everything this session changed).\n2. The host session picks the **three lenses** that matter most for *these* changes.\n3. It spawns **three subagents**, one per lens. They did not author the changes, so they carry\n   none of the session's bias.\n4. Each reviews and reports findings.\n5. The host **triages** each finding — accept or decline, with a reason. Declining is legitimate.\n6. The host fixes what it accepted.\n7. New round: fresh reviewers, told what was applied and what was declined and why.\n8. Stop when a round finds nothing worth fixing. A round cap bounds the loop.\n\nOutput: the improved changes plus a ledger of every finding and its verdict.\n\n## 2. Provenance — this is a promotion, not a new invention\n\nThe flow is `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md`, the architect's own review discipline, hardened\nacross the v11–v28 ships. Wrong layer —\n  the pattern is domain-agnostic (used on doc sweeps and lore edits, not just code). Ships as a\n  core plugin skill; DF/AIQA becomes a consumer.\n- **The loop is first-class.** The backlog treated iteration as \"address-then-decide-if-another-round.\"\n  Here the loop, its termination rule, and the dispositions ledger are the specified core.\n\n## 3. Naming\n\n`trilens-loop` — `tri` (three perspectives) + `lens` (the framework's existing word for a review\nperspective, from `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md`) + `loop` (the iteration is the feature).\n\n- Claude Code: `/lr:trilens-loop` · Cursor: `/lr-trilens-loop` · Codex: `/lr:trilens-loop`\n- Canonical folder `skills/trilens-loop/`, doc `docs/trilens-loop.md`.\n- Distinctive enough to avoid confusion with Claude Code's built-in `/review`, `/code-review`,\n  `/security-review`.\n- Rejected: `/lr:review` (generic, collides conceptually with engine built-ins), `/lr:trilens`\n  (drops the loop), `/lr:review-cycle` (drops the three lenses), `/lr:converge` (names the goal,\n  not the mechanism), `/lr:triad` (collides with `positioning-triad-differentiation.md` vocabulary).\n\n## 4. The load-bearing decision: subagent-as-semantics\n\n**In every existing fan-out site — recall, consult, merge — the subagent is an _optimization_:**\nparallelism plus context isolation. Serial host-side execution reaches the same answer, just\nslower. That is why Cursor's conservative serial override (`docs/engines/cursor.md`) is a lossless\ndegradation everywhere it currently applies.\n\n**`trilens-loop` is the first skill where the subagent is the _semantics_.** Independence from the\nauthor's bias *is* the deliverable. A host reviewing its own work is not a slow review — it is not\na review. So the usual graceful-degradation-to-serial escape hatch is **unavailable here**.\n\nConsequences:\n\n- The skill requires a **native engine subagent mechanism**. No host-side fallback.\n- A headless shell-out (`cursor-agent -p`, `claude -p`, `codex exec`) would also give independence,\n  and was considered — the user explicitly rejected workarounds in favour of native subagents.\n- On an engine with no verified subagent mechanism, the skill reports that plainly and stops rather\n  than pretending. It does **not** silently degrade into self-review.\n\nCandidate lore topic: `subagent-as-optimization-vs-subagent-as-semantics.md` — a new axis on the\nengine-degradation question, and the first case where a binding's conservative fallback is\nfeature-destroying rather than merely slower.\n\n## 5. Per-engine subagent binding\n\n| Engine | Mechanism | Read-only enforcement | Status |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Claude Code | `Agent` tool, `subagent_type: general-purpose`, 3 parallel calls in one message | by instruction | verified in practice (v11–v28 ships) |\n| Codex | `spawn_agent`, role `explorer`; collect with `wait_agent` | structural (`explorer` is read-only) | verified end-to-end (v19) |\n| Cursor | `Task` tool (subagents shipped in Cursor 2.4; editor + CLI + Cloud) | `readonly: true` in an agent definition | **documented, not yet validated by us** |\n\nHard rules:\n\n- **Never a context-inheriting fork.** On Claude that means never `subagent_type: \"fork\"` — a fork\n  inherits the host's conversation and with it the bias the skill exists to remove.\n- **Reviewers are read-only.** They report; they never edit. The host owns all writes. Prefer a\n  structurally read-only role where the engine has one; otherwise instruct it.\n- Codex `agents.max_depth = 1` and `max_threads ≈ 6` both accommodate three reviewers.\n\n### Cursor — what changed and what is still open\n\nThe shipped `docs/engines/cursor.md` binding says *\"no verified Cursor-native in-session subagent\nmechanism is relied on by the framework… do not claim parallel fan-out on Cursor until it is\nvalidated on the real engine.\"* That is **epistemic conservatism, not a discovered limitation** —\nthe probe notes (`cursor-agent-cli-probe-findings.md`,\n`cursor-cli-and-harness-operational-notes.md`) contain zero mentions of subagents. The question was\nnever asked, because the serial override was sufficient for every skill that existed at the time\n(19/19 lifecycle green at v20).\n\nEstablished 2026-07-25 from Cursor's own docs:\n\n- Subagents shipped in **Cursor 2.4**, available in *\"the editor, CLI, and Cloud Agents.\"*\n- The agent spawns them with a **`Task` tool**; several run in parallel; one extra nesting level.\n- Definitions are markdown + YAML frontmatter in `.cursor/agents/` — and Cursor **also reads\n  `.claude/agents/` and `.codex/agents/`**.\n- Frontmatter carries `name`, `description`, `model`, `readonly`, `is_background`.\n- CLI support was **broken by a bug** (the `Task` tool was not injected into the CLI toolset, so\n  subagents ran serially in one context) — fixed for standard accounts around **2026-03-02**. Our\n  Cursor profile was written 2026-07-05, *after* the fix; it was simply never rechecked.\n- `cursor-agent --help` (2026.07.20-8cc9c0b) shows no spawn flag — expected, since `Task` is a\n  model-facing in-session tool, exactly like Codex's `spawn_agent`.\n\n**Open, to be settled by the lifecycle test:** whether Cursor's `Task` tool accepts a **free-text\nbrief** (like Claude's `Agent`) or only dispatches a **pre-defined agent file by name**. The skill\nneeds free-text briefs — lenses are chosen per change, so they cannot be pre-declared. If Cursor\nturns out to be name-only, the fallback is for the skill to write three throwaway definitions into\n`.cursor/agents/` before dispatch and remove them after. Deliberately unresolved in the doc until\nmeasured; the doc marks the Cursor path as unvalidated.\n\n## 6. Interface\n\n```\n/lr:trilens-loop [free-text amendments]\n```\n\n**No flags.** One optional free-text argument, consistent with `/lr:recall [hint]` and\n`/lr:consult <agent> [hint]`. Free text works identically on every engine, where flag parsing does\nnot, and it lets the user amend anything without the doc having to anticipate it.\n\n```\n/lr:trilens-loop\n/lr:trilens-loop only one round, no fixes\n/lr:trilens-loop use security, performance and API compatibility as the lenses\n/lr:trilens-loop review only the changes under tests/\n/lr:trilens-loop max 5 rounds\n/lr:trilens-loop five lenses instead of three\n```\n\n**Free text is genuinely free** (user decision, 2026-07-25): it may override anything, including\nthe round cap, the lens count, and the independence rule. The one obligation is **visibility** —\nwhen an amendment switches off a default rail, the skill says so out loud in its output rather\nthan silently complying. Rails that are quietly removed are worse than rails that were never there.\n\n## 7. Default scope\n\nDefault = **everything this session changed**, not a git range. The anchor is *what we did here*.\n\nResolution order:\n\n1. The files the host edited or created this session (the host knows these directly).\n2. Cross-check with `git status --porcelain` and `git diff` in each repo those files live in, to\n   catch changes the host forgot and to produce reviewable diffs.\n3. If the session also committed, include those commits.\n4. State the resolved scope before dispatching, so a wrong scope is caught before three agents are\n   paid for.\n\nMulti-repo sessions are normal in a lore workspace (plugin + dev repo). Scope may span repos; group\nthe file list by repo. Free text can narrow or replace the scope entirely.\n\n## 8. Lens selection\n\nThe host picks three lenses from the change and the session, not from a fixed list.\n\nRules (inherited from `parallel-reviewer-fanout-pattern.md`):\n\n- **Mutually exclusive.** If two reviewers would find the same issues, a slot is wasted.\n- **Tell each what to skip**, including what the other lenses own.\n- **The don't-fan-out test:** *would a single agent looking at all N items produce the same verdicts\n  as N separate agents?* If yes, batch instead — a fan-out over items that share a uniform property\n  adds cost without adding rejection power.\n- Starter catalog (a menu, not a default): correctness, security / adversarial input, UX &\n  discoverability, framework architectural consistency, terminology coherence, newcomer experience,\n  release readiness, AI-installer literal-execution, filesystem-grounded verification.\n- The architecture lens gets pointed at the booted agent's `lore-context.md` as a baseline, so it\n  can apply the agent's own stated meta-rules to the change.\n\n## 9. Brief shape — goal, not rationale\n\nEach reviewer brief carries: context (what changed and **what it is for**), the file list with\nabsolute paths, what to look for under this lens, what to skip, and the output format.\n\n**Deliberate omission: the host's rationale for each choice.** The reviewer gets the *goal* — enough\nto judge fitness for purpose — but not the argument for why each decision was made. Rationale\npre-empts the criticism the fan-out is being paid to produce. This is the bias-minimisation rule at\nthe brief level, the counterpart to not inheriting session context at the spawn level.\n\nCandidate lore topic: `brief-the-goal-not-the-rationale.md`.\n\nOutput format required from every reviewer:\n\n- Findings: severity (`BLOCKER` / `HIGH` / `MEDIUM` / `LOW`), `file:line`, the issue, a\n  one-sentence fix.\n- A closing **verdict line**: `SHIP`, `SHIP-WITH-FIXES`, or `BLOCK`.\n- ≤600 words, to keep three reports digestible.\n\n## 10. Round protocol\n\n**Round 1** — breadth. Three reviewers in parallel, lens-isolated.\n\n**Triage** — the host's job, and the one place authorship is an advantage:\n\n- **Verify every `BLOCKER` and `HIGH` against the actual file before acting.** Reviewers\n  occasionally hallucinate or work from stale state.\n- Accept or decline each finding, each with a one-line reason. Declining is legitimate.\n- Cross-check overlap: two lenses hitting one issue from different angles is one fix, noted twice.\n- Fix what was accepted.\n\n**Round N+1** — fresh reviewers, because independence is the point. Fresh reviewers have no memory\nof the previous round, so each brief **must** open with:\n\n1. A numbered **APPLIED / DECLINED (+reason)** ledger of *that lens's own* prior findings.\n2. A short digest of what the *other* lenses' fixes changed in this lens's territory.\n\nWithout that ledger, declined findings resurface every round and the loop never converges. With it,\nacross a validated four-round run, no reviewer re-raised a settled item.\n\nExpect round N's findings to cluster on round N−1's **edits** — fixes create new seams. That is\nnormal convergence behaviour, not failure.\n\n**Optional final round** — when the loop is already converging, the last round may be a *single*\nreviewer with the full diff and filesystem access, rather than three lens-isolated ones. Round 1 and\nthe final round are different jobs: breadth-parallel-isolated versus depth-sequential-whole. A\nsingle full-diff reviewer catches cross-file drift that per-lens reviewers structurally cannot.\n\n## 11. Termination\n\nStop when a round produces nothing worth fixing. Convergence by shrinking findings count is the\nsignal — typical runs converge in 2–3 rounds; large cross-cutting changes have taken 7.\n\nTwo guards:\n\n- **Round cap, default 3.** Amendable by free text. Prevents an unattended loop from burning agents.\n- **Reviewer-gated stop.** The loop may not terminate while any reviewer's most recent verdict is\n  `BLOCK`. The host may still override — it must say so explicitly, with a reason. Without this the\n  same session both writes the fixes and grades its own convergence; the guard makes termination\n  partly reviewer-owned.\n\nOn hitting the cap with findings still open, report what remains rather than declaring success.\n\n## 12. Degradation\n\n- **Partial returns are additive evidence.** A reviewer that returns one finding then stalls has\n  done useful work. Do not discard the round; read what came back.\n- **Fewer than three returning is still a round** — continue, and say which lens is missing.\n- **No native subagent mechanism → stop and report.** Never self-review under the skill's name.\n\n## 13. Boundaries\n\n- Not `/lr:check` (domain content consistency) and not `/lr:doctor` (runtime ailments) — those are\n  state-scoped; this is change-scoped.\n- Not part of finalization. It **never writes lore**, never reflects, never merges, never commits.\n- It *does* edit the files under review — that is the point of the loop. `--report-only` behaviour\n  is available through free text (\"no fixes\").\n- No persistence in the MVP: the ledger is printed, not filed. Persisting review records is a\n  possible follow-up.\n\n## 14. Cost\n\nThree subagents per round, 2–3 rounds typical. Real but proportionate for a substantive change; not\nfor a one-line edit. The doc says so, so the skill is not reached for reflexively.\n\n## 15. Ship scope\n\nPlugin (`lore-framework`):\n\n- `skills/trilens-loop/SKILL.md` — thin pointer.\n- `docs/trilens-loop.md` — all logic.\n- `.cursor-skills/lr-trilens-loop/SKILL.md` — generated by `scripts/sync-cursor-skills`.\n- `docs/engines/cursor.md` — subagent-spawn binding updated (stale since 2.4).\n- `README.md` — skill table entry.\n- **No `VERSION` bump and no manifest bumps** — this increment merges into a bigger release later.\n  Cache-clear applies at that release, not here.\n\nDev (`lore-framework-dev`):\n\n- A lifecycle scenario covering this skill only.\n- This draft.\n- The release-notes increment, held here until the bigger release absorbs it.\n\n## 16. Open seams\n\n- **Cursor `Task` free-text vs named-definition** (§5) — settled by the lifecycle test.\n- **Persisting the ledger** — out of MVP scope; revisit if review records prove worth keeping.\n- **Reviewer model tier** — inherits the session default; no knob. Free text can ask for a tier\n  where the engine supports it.\n- **Interaction with `/lr:spawn-teammate`** — named teammates do not auto-return their reports, so a\n  teammate-based variant would need explicit send-back instructions. Out of scope; the skill uses\n  plain background subagents.\n"

The choice\ndepends on the change and the project — it is a judgement call, not a lookup.\n\nRules:\n\n- **Mutually exclusive.** If two reviewers would surface the same issues, you have wasted a slot.\n- **Tell each reviewer what to skip**, including what the other two lenses own.\n- **Apply the don't-fan-out test:** *would a single agent looking at all N items produce the same\n  verdicts as N separate agents?* If yes, batch that work into one lens instead. A fan-out across\n  items sharing a uniform property adds cost without adding rejection power.\n\nA starter catalog to choose from — a menu, not a default:\n\n| Lens | Looks for |\n|---|---|\n| Correctness | bugs, races, edge cases, wrong assumptions |\n| Security / adversarial input | hostile input, injection, path traversal, unsafe parsing |\n| UX & discoverability | will a real user find this, understand it, and act on it |\n| Architectural consistency | does it compose with the project's own stated patterns |\n| Terminology coherence | did a vocabulary change hold together across every site |\n| Newcomer experience | walk it as a first-time reader; where does the path break |\n| Release readiness | breaking changes disclosed, versions and stamps consistent |\n| Literal-execution fidelity | read it as the agent that must execute it word for word |\n| Filesystem-grounded verification | run the commands the change promises; verify claims live |\n\nWhen one lens is architectural and a lore agent is booted, point that reviewer at the agent's\n`lore-context.md` as its baseline, so it applies the agent's own stated meta-rules to the change.\n\nAnnounce the three chosen lenses and one line of reasoning each before dispatching.\n\n### Step 3: Spawn three independent reviewers\n\n> **Engine note.** Use your engine profile's **subagent-spawn** binding\n> (`<framework-root>/docs/engines/<engine>.md`). Include anything adjacent that is *not* in\n   scope but might need a look; that boundary is where misses hide.\n3. **This lens** — what to look for, with example failure modes.\n4. **What to skip** — nits, generic advice, low-confidence guesses, and explicitly what the other\n   two lenses are covering.\n5. **Output format** — findings as severity (`BLOCKER` / `HIGH` / `MEDIUM` / `LOW`), `file:line`,\n   the issue, a one-sentence fix; then a closing **verdict line**: `SHIP`, `SHIP-WITH-FIXES`, or\n   `BLOCK`. Order the work `BLOCKER` → `HIGH` → `MEDIUM`, then judge the `LOW`s.\n\nMaintain a **dispositions ledger** across the whole loop: for each finding, its lens, its severity,\nand `APPLIED` or `DECLINED` with the reason. The ledger drives the next round and is part of the\nfinal output.\n\n### Step 5: Apply the accepted fixes\n\nThe host makes every edit. Keep the fixes scoped to what was accepted — a review round is not a\nlicence for unrelated refactoring.\n\nIf a finding is real but genuinely out of scope, record it as `DECLINED (out of scope)` and say so\nin the final summary, so it is visible rather than lost.\n\n### Step 6: Next round, or stop\n\nIf anything was fixed, run another round. Spawn **fresh** reviewers — independence is the point, so\nthey start cold with no memory of the previous round. That memory has to be supplied deliberately:\n\nEach round-N+1 brief **must** open with:\n\n1. A numbered **APPLIED / DECLINED (+reason)** list of *that lens's own* findings from the previous\n   round.\n2. A short digest of what the *other* lenses' fixes changed inside this lens's territory.\n\nWithout that ledger, declined findings resurface every round and the loop never converges. With it,\nsettled items stay settled and each round reads as a delta rather than a re-review.\n\nExpect round N's findings to cluster around round N−1's **edits** — fixes create new seams. That is\nnormal convergence behaviour, not a sign the process is failing.\n\n**Optional shape for the last round.** Once the loop is clearly converging, the final round may be a\n*single* reviewer given the whole diff and filesystem access, instead of three lens-isolated ones.\nRound 1 and the final round are different jobs: breadth, parallel and isolated versus depth,\nsequential and whole. One reviewer holding the entire diff catches cross-file drift that per-lens\nreviewers structurally cannot see.\n\n## Termination\n\nStop when a round produces nothing worth fixing. Shrinking findings counts are the signal —\ntwo to three rounds is typical; a change that reshapes several cross-cutting files can take more.\n\nTwo guards:\n\n- **Round cap — default 3.** Amendable by free text. It stops an unattended loop from spending\n  agents indefinitely.\n- **Reviewer-gated stop.** Do not terminate while any reviewer's most recent verdict is `BLOCK`.\n  You may override that — but state the override and the reason explicitly. Without this guard the\n  same session writes the fixes *and* grades its own convergence; the guard puts part of the\n  stopping decision in the reviewers' hands.\n\nIf the cap is reached with findings still open, **report what remains** instead of declaring\nsuccess.\n\n## Final output\n\nReport:\n\n1. The scope that was reviewed.\n2. The lenses used each round, and each reviewer's verdict.\n3. The dispositions ledger — every finding, its severity, `APPLIED` or `DECLINED` with the reason.\n4. What was fixed.\n5. Anything still open, deferred, or overridden — including any rail the user's free text switched\n   off.\n\nThe ledger is printed, not filed. This skill writes no records of its own.\n\n## When reviewers fall short\n\n- **A partial return is additive evidence.** A reviewer that reports one finding and then stalls has\n  done useful work. Read what came back; do not discard the round.\n- **Fewer than three returning is still a round.** Continue, and say which lens is missing so the\n  gap is visible.\n- **Reviewers may disagree with each other.** Decide yourself, after reading the actual files.\n  Reviewers work from limited context; consensus is not the standard, correctness is.\n- **If the engine cannot spawn subagents, stop.** Report the limitation. Never substitute\n  self-review.\n\n## Boundaries\n\n- **Not `/lr:check`** — that checks the domain's own content consistency. **Not `/lr:doctor`** — that\n  diagnoses runtime ailments. Both are state-scoped; this is change-scoped.\n- **Not part of finalization.** It never writes lore, never reflects, never merges, never commits.\n- It **does** edit the files under review — that is the loop. For a review with no edits, say so in\n  the free text (\"no fixes\").\n\n## See also\n\n- `<framework-root>/docs/engines/claude.md`, `codex.md`, `cursor.md` — the subagent-spawn bindings\n  this skill depends on.\n- `<framework-root>/docs/conventions.md` § Tooling: CWD Safety — why scope resolution uses\n  `git -C <repo>`.\n- `<framework-root>/docs/check.md` — domain content consistency, the state-scoped sibling.\n- `<framework-root>/docs/spawn-teammate.md` — named teammates do not auto-return reports; this skill\n  deliberately uses plain background subagents instead.\n"
}
````

def build_cursor_takeover_paths(tmpdir, workspace):
    """Cursor HOME + JSONL transcript for /lr:takeover lifecycle scenarios."""
    import sys

    fixtures = os.path.join(HERE, "..", "fixtures")
    if fixtures not in sys.path:
        sys.path.insert(0, fixtures)
    from cursor_takeover_fixture import minimal_session

    digest_path = os.path.join(workspace, "takeover-digest.md")
    return cursor_home, jsonl_path, digest_path


def make_origin_ahead(fx):
    """Push one extra commit to origin via a second clone, so origin is ahead."""
    clone2 = os.path.join(fx.root, "clone2")
    subprocess.run(["git", "clone", fx.origin, clone2],
                   capture_output=True, text=True, check=True)
    topic = os.path.join(clone2, "agents", AGENT_NAME, "lore", "fresh-fact.md")
    with open(topic, "w") as f:
        f.write(f"# Fresh Fact\n\nFreshness canary: {FRESH_CANARY}.\n")
    _commit_all(clone2, "fixture: teammate pushed a fresh topic")
    _git(clone2, "push", "origin", "main")

os\nimport shutil\nimport sys\nimport tempfile\nimport unittest\n\nsys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)))\nfrom harness import (\n    REVIEW_DEFECT_CANARY,\n    SKIP_REASON,\n TrilensLoopScenarios(unittest.TestCase):\n\n    def setUp(self):\n        tmp = tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix=\"lr-lifecycle-\")\n        self.addCleanup(shutil.rmtree, tmp, ignore_errors=True)\n        self.fx = build_fixture(tmp)\n        self.target = plant_reviewable_change(self.fx)\n        with open(self.target) as f:\n            self.before = f.read()\n\n    def _target_now(self):\n        with open(self.target) as f:\n            return f.read()\n\n    def _assert_three_lenses(self, text):\n        \"\"\"The LENSES: line must name three distinct lenses, not one or a [p.strip() for p in lines[-1].split(\":\", 1)[1].split(\"|\") if p.strip()]\n        self.assertGreaterEqual(\n            len(lenses), 3,\n            f\"expected three lenses, got {lenses!r} from line {lines[-1]!r}\",\n        )\n        self.assertEqual(\n            len(lenses), len(set(l.lower() for l in lenses)),\n            f\"lenses must be distinct, got {lenses!r}\",\n        )\n\n    def test_24_trilens_loop_single_round_finds_and_fixes(self):\n        \"\"\"One round with fixes: three lenses chosen, planted dangling ref found, target edited.\"\"\"\n        r = run_engine(self.fx.workspace, TRILENS_PROMPT)\n        print(f\"\\n  [{self.id().split('.')[-1]}] {r.summary()}\")\n        self.assertEqual(r.exit_code, 0, f\"engine run failed: REVIEW_DEFECT_CANARY, r.text,\n            f\"reviewers did not surface the planted dangling reference:\\n{r.text}\",\n        )\n        self.assertNotEqual(\n            self.before, self._target_now(),\n            \"fixes were allowed but the review target was never edited\",\n        )\n\n    def test_25_trilens_loop_report_only_makes_no_edits(self):\n        \"\"\"Report-only amendment: finding still surfaced, but no file is touched.\"\"\"\n        r = run_engine(self.fx.workspace, TRILENS_REPORT_ONLY_PROMPT)\n        print(f\"\\n  [{self.id().split('.')[-1]}] {r.summary()}\")\n        self.assertEqual(r.exit_code, 0, f\"engine run failed: {r.stderr[-500:]}\")\n        self.assertIn(\"TRILENS-DONE\", r.text, f\"skill did not run to completion:\\n{r.text}\")\n        self.assertIn(\n            REVIEW_DEFECT_CANARY, r.text,\n            f\"reviewers did not surface the planted dangling reference:\\n{r.text}\",\n        )\n        self.assertEqual(\n            self.before, self._target_now(),\n            \"report-only was requested but the review target was modified\",\n        )\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    unittest.main()\n"
}

Lenses must be\n  mutually exclusive, and each brief tells its reviewer what the other two lenses own.\n- **Reviewers are independent subagents.** They did not author the changes, they start cold, and\n  they are read-only — they report, the host fixes. Free text may\n  amend anything, including the rails; when it switches a rail off, the skill says so in its output\n  rather than complying silently.\n\nThe skill is change-scoped, which is what separates it from its state-scoped siblings: `/lr:check`\nchecks a domain's content consistency, `/lr:doctor` diagnoses runtime ailments. It is not part of\nfinalization — it never writes lore, never reflects, never merges, never commits.\n\n### Cursor's subagent binding, corrected\n\n`docs/engines/cursor.md` carried a conservative binding stating that no Cursor-native in-session\nsubagent mechanism was relied on. That was epistemic caution rather than a discovered limitation,\nand it had gone stale: Cursor shipped subagents in **2.4** (editor, CLI and Cloud Agents),\ndispatched via a `Task` tool, and the CLI bug that hid `Task` from the toolset was fixed around\n2026-03-02 — before the profile was written.\n\nThe binding now keeps serial host-side execution as the validated default for the procedures that\nalready pass that way (recall, consult, attach, merge, conflict resolution), and carves out one\nexception: procedures where **subagent independence is the semantics rather than an optimization**\nuse `Task`. `trilens-loop` is the only such procedure today, and the reason the carve-out exists —\nserial host-side execution of a bias-removal review is not a slower version of the procedure, it is\nan invalid one.\n\nA new § Native subagents section records what Cursor documents versus what the framework has\nactually validated, and flags the one load-bearing unknown: whether `Task` accepts a free-text brief\nor only dispatches pre-defined agent files by name. Procedures using `Task` on Cursor handle the\nname-only case by writing throwaway `readonly` definitions under `.cursor/agents/` and deleting them\nafterwards.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Lifecycle scenarios 24–25 (`tests/lifecycle/test_trilens_loop.py`) cover the skill end-to-end on a\n  real engine: a planted uncommitted lore topic with a dangling cross-reference is reviewed in a\n  single round.   "description": "Read termination through boundaries"
}
```

#### Result

```text
## Termination

Stop when a round produces nothing worth fixing. Shrinking findings counts are the signal —
two to three rounds is typical; a change that reshapes several cross-cutting files can take more.

Two guards:

- **Round cap — default 3.** Amendable by free text. It stops an unattended loop from spending
  agents indefinitely.
- **Reviewer-gated stop.** Do not terminate while any reviewer's most recent verdict is `BLOCK`.
  You may override that — but state the override and the reason explicitly. Without this guard the
  same session writes the fixes *and* grades its own convergence; the guard puts part of the
  stopping decision in the reviewers' hands.

If the cap is reached with findings still open, **report what remains** instead of declaring
success.

## Final output

Report:

1. The scope that was reviewed.
2. The lenses used each round, and each reviewer's verdict.
3. The dispositions ledger — every finding, its severity, `APPLIED` or `DECLINED` with the reason.
4. What was fixed.
5. Anything still open, deferred, or overridden — including any rail the user's free text switched
   off.

The ledger is printed, not filed. This skill writes no records of its own.

## When reviewers fall short

- **A partial return is additive evidence.** A reviewer that reports one finding and then stalls has
  done useful work. Read what came back; do not discard the round.
- **Fewer than three returning is still a round.** Continue, and say which lens is missing so the
  gap is visible.
- **Reviewers may disagree with each other.** Decide yourself, after reading the actual files.
  Reviewers work from limited context; consensus is not the standard, correctness is.
- **If the engine cannot spawn subagents, stop.** Report the limitation. Never substitute
  self-review.

## Boundaries
```
